On an Intel Mac use a device: the simulator cannot run the library (Platform Notes)

The connect time budget

connectTimeout (30 seconds by default) or ConnectOptions.timeout covers scanning, opening the link and the retries between attempts. When connect fails on it:

  • It returns at once, with the error of the last failed attempt. If no attempt failed, it returns ConnectFailed if a scan found the car or a remembered target was being opened, and ScanTimeout if no advertisement was found. The Go original returns the last error too, otherwise one generic deadline error for all of these.
  • It does not wait for an attempt still running. That attempt is cancelled and cleans up after connect has returned, waiting at most 5 seconds for the platform disconnect. If it still opens a link, the library disconnects that link.
  • The only wait past the budget: a connection created at the very moment the budget ran out is closed before connect returns, which also takes at most 5 seconds. So the library itself adds at most 5 seconds to a failed connect.
  • The budget is a timer, and a timer cannot fire while the app is not running, so a suspended app can overrun by any amount. On a real iPhone a 30-second budget ended 36 minutes late (docs/analysis/2026-11-01-build18-link-health.md F3, likely, not confirmed). No timeout can prevent this.

Seen once on a real iPhone: ConnectFailed after 32.63 seconds. The likely cause is a late timer, not confirmed because the log records no process state. ScanTimeout returns, where no attempt had failed, overran the same way (at 31.35, 32.40 and 33.19 seconds; that build, before #140, also returned ScanTimeout after finding the car), and the app's next 4-second wait took 9.03 seconds. The log cannot exclude the one library wait above, which would need a link to open at the instant the budget ran out. No harm was seen (#130).

Frames without a source

Decision (#130): a frame without a source stays out of vehicle.events and remains a [drop:missing-source] line in the observation log.

  • It is not a plain broadcast (14, B2), and the Go original drops it too. Without a source nothing says the frame came from the security domain: reinterpreted=… only shows how the same bytes read as a FromVCSECMessage.
  • Delivering these frames would widen the public contract: raw is always a whole RoutableMessage frame, and any stray frame would become an event.
  • The gain is small. A real car sent 2 such frames in 12 connections. The authenticationRequest (DRIVE, IDENTIFICATION) arrived 1 ms before the app saw connected, so nothing was collecting yet and, with no replay, a wider rule would not have delivered it. The other was a bare commandStatus, which has no event type. An app also cannot act on an AuthenticationRequest: the library does not send AuthenticationResponse.

Reconsider this if a car sends such frames often: reports elsewhere describe one about every 1.1 seconds (14, B3.2).

Debug logging

  • Pass a logger in TeslaBleConfig. The default logs nothing.
  • DebugLogger() writes to Logcat on Android, the unified log on iOS and java.util.logging on the JVM. Use it in development builds only.
  • To send logs elsewhere, implement TeslaLogger: one function receiving a level, a tag and a lazily built message. A logger that throws does not stop the library.
  • Levels: DEBUG (raw bytes sent and received), INFO (connections and sessions), WARN (dropped or discarded messages), ERROR (situations the library cannot recover from).
What never appears in logs

The library masks VINs and never logs private keys, session keys, nonces or decrypted content. Keep it that way in your own logs: do not print vin.value, and mask VINs before sharing logs. Do not log getState results either, toString() included: a VehicleData can hold the location and the destination, and InfotainmentRejected.message carries the car's own text.

To report a problem, use the iOS sample's "copy whole result" button log together with DebugLogger. There is no probe or diagnostics entry point.

Observation log and vehicle.events

Both show the car's broadcasts, for different readers:

Observation logvehicle.events
What it isOne DEBUG text line per dropped message or reply to your own request, tag observe (it also has connect scan hits, see the next section)A public Flow<VehicleEvent> on Vehicle (FR-035)
Which framesEvery dropped frame (replies to other phones, unreadable frames, broadcasts) and every reply to your own requests, in both domainsOnly plain broadcasts (14, B2)
ContentA summary for people; values of unknown fields only in the detailed formTyped events, unknownFields paths, and the whole frame in raw
VINMasked by the libraryNot masked in raw; the app masks it before storing or sharing
NeedsA logger that keeps DEBUG (development builds)Nothing; it works in release builds
Use it forExploring the protocol, reporting problemsApp features such as live state, and collecting raw frames for analysis
  • When one frame carries both vehicleStatus and authenticationRequest, the observe line shows only the one that comes last: the schema puts both in one oneof, and an ordinary decode keeps only the last one. vehicle.events reads both fields and delivers both events (Status, then AuthenticationRequest).
  • Do not parse observe lines in app code: their format is for development and may change. Use vehicle.events.

Observation log (development)

While you develop, the library can write one summary line for every message it drops and for every reply to one of its own requests, in both domains. It shows what the car sends without being asked and how its replies line up with requests. It changes no behaviour: broadcasts are still kept out of reply matching, and the plain ones also reach apps through vehicle.events (previous section). What each pushed message means is in 14. Reading Vehicle State, Part B. The Bluetooth transport adds one line for every scan hit of connect.

  • Tag observe, level DEBUG. A logger that discards DEBUG never builds the line. Visible through DebugLogger() or your own TeslaLogger. The iOS sample's observation mode collects only these lines.
  • Shape: [point] NB from=… to=… request_uuid=… payload=…, where NB is the size of the received frame. Reply lines end with attempt=N auth=….
  • Scan hit line (FR-038): [connect:scan-hit] after=<ms>ms rssi=<dBm|unavailable> connectable=<true|false|unknown> attempt=<n>. One line for every advertisement found by a scan that connect runs itself, written before the connectable check and before opening, so it also appears for a connect that fails and for each retry. after is the time since connect started (the same measure as hitAfterMillis, 12. Getting Started), attempt is the open attempt the hit was found for. The time your logger receives the line is the wall-clock time of the hit. ble.scan(vin) and a target reused without a scan write no line. It has no advertised name and no device identifier.
PartValues
[drop:unparseable]The frame did not decode as a routable message. The line ends with reinterpreted=… (or undecodable)
[drop:missing-source]No source. The line ends with reinterpreted=…: the same bytes read as an unwrapped FromVCSECMessage. Never an event (Frames without a source)
[drop:missing-destination]No destination (not seen from the real car)
[drop:to-domain]Addressed to a domain, not to a client address (the car's broadcasts use DOMAIN_BROADCAST)
[drop:malformed]Wrong request UUID length or wrong address length
[drop:no-handler]A valid address, but no open request owns it. A reply to a recently closed request reads to=address(16B, closed) and ends with attempt=N (see Reply lines below)
[drop:handler-closed], [drop:queue-full]The matching request closed meanwhile, or its answer queue was full. The reply line of the same frame comes first
[reply:vcsec], [reply:infotainment]A security-domain or infotainment-domain reply to an open request, one line per received frame (see Reply lines below)
from=, to=domain(DOMAIN_X), address(16B, self|open-request|closed|other) (to= only), none, empty
payload=FromVCSECMessage{…}, Response{fields=[…]}, encrypted(NB), NB, none
unknownFields=[n]Field numbers on the wire that the generated protocol does not know
uiDesire=, gear=Vehicle status fields 6 and 7, named by the extended protocol definitions (GEAR_UNKNOWN, GEAR_PARK, GEAR_DRIVE, GEAR_REVERSE, GEAR_NEUTRAL). gear is confirmed on a real car (see 14. Reading Vehicle State); the meaning of uiDesire is not. Always printed, because a missing enum reads as its zero value
authenticationRequest{requestedLevel=…, reasons=[…], token=NB}FromVCSECMessage field 3, named by the extended protocol definitions (field names unverified). The token is shown by length only
absent=[…]Parts of the vehicle status the car did not send (closureStatuses, detailedClosureStatus)
key=sha1:xxxxxxxxA public key: only the first 4 bytes of its SHA-1

Reply lines

The library writes one [reply:vcsec] or [reply:infotainment] line per received frame, after the decrypt step, so a reply that step dropped has its line too. The line ends with attempt=N auth=…. If the frame then does not reach the caller, a [drop:handler-closed] or [drop:queue-full] line follows. A frame whose processing is interrupted by the app disconnecting gets no line and is not delivered, so a missing line does not prove that no reply came.

PartMeaning
attempt=NAn opaque number for the request on this connection, starting at 1. Every request takes a new number, including a handshake request and each retry, so one command can use several. Compare numbers only within one connection: a new connection starts at 1 again
auth=gcm-okAES_GCM_Response_data is present, the tag verified and the counter passed the replay check of the request. Delivered
auth=gcm-failedAES_GCM_Response_data is present and verification failed. Dropped
auth=gcm-replayThe tag verified, but the replay window rejected the counter: already seen, or older than the window. Dropped
auth=gcm-no-sessionAES_GCM_Response_data is present, but there is no session to check it with (no private key, or the session is not established). Dropped
auth=noneNo AES_GCM_Response_data, so nothing was checked. Delivered. A session info reply reads this too: its HMAC tag is verified separately and this line does not report that
  • auth= reports the payload check, not a verdict on the reply. AES-GCM covers the payload and signed_message_fault. The outer operation_status, the first half of status=, is outside it, so auth=gcm-ok does not cover it (ADR-0015).
  • The line is built from the frame as received, never from the decrypted message (FR-112). An encrypted reply reads payload=encrypted(NB) whatever auth= says, and encrypted(NB) alone only means the signature field is there.
  • to=address(16B, closed) is a correspondence and nothing more. The reply's key (routing address, plus the request UUID for infotainment) matches a request this connection closed, and attempt=N names it. It does not show that the reply is genuine or that the request succeeded: the library does not decrypt such a reply, which leaves the replay check of the request untouched, and does not deliver it. That is why the line has no auth=.
  • The history holds 32 closed requests of the connection, and the entry that went in first falls out first. A closed request enters at the library's next cleanup, oldest attempt first within one cleanup, so the order follows closing closely but not exactly. A late reply to a request no longer in the history reads other in the security domain and self in the infotainment domain, where all requests share one address.
  • The history empties when the connection ends, whether the link dropped or the app disconnected. Nothing carries over to a new connection, not even a request that was still open when the link dropped and closed later.

Detail form (exploration only)

Example: unknownFields=[48:len(8){1:varint=3(0x3), 2:len(4)"ABCD"}, 9]. Entries keep their arrival order and repeated fields repeat. The field number and wire type are kept; no field name is invented.

Wire typeShown as
varintvarint=<unsigned decimal>(0x<hex>)
fixed32 / fixed64fixed32=0x + 8 hex digits, fixed64=0x + 16 hex digits
LENlen(N){…} when the whole range parses as protobuf (depth 3, 64 fields per message, 256 bytes at most). Otherwise len(N)"text" for printable ASCII (control characters escaped), otherwise len(N)0x… hex
Over a limit or corruptlen(N, truncated) or len(N, invalid)
Key-like LENExactly 16, 32, 33, 64 or 65 bytes: redacted(NB). A 65-byte value that starts with 0x04: sha1:xxxxxxxx

VINs (17 characters) are masked in text and in raw bytes before any hex is produced; only the first 3 and last 4 characters stay. Masking runs on the raw bytes before they are parsed, so masked bytes can change the value or structure that is shown (nothing leaks). This output is for exploration only. It does not tell what a field means. On the real car a missing closureStatuses meant all closed for the driver doors, the rear trunk and the charge port, but a trunk operated by the interior button produced no broadcast at all, so a broadcast alone cannot prove the trunk is closed (see 14, Part B).

Limits and rules

  • Detailed decoding is a narrow exception, and only for frames the car originally sent as plaintext (never for anything the library decrypted, so FR-112 is unaffected); vehicle.events hands such frames to apps as raw, decodable or not. All of these must hold: from the security domain, to the broadcast domain, empty request UUID, no signature, no session info, and a payload that reads as FromVCSECMessage. Then only FromVCSECMessage unknown fields 48 and 53 and VehicleStatus unknown fields 7 and 8 are decoded (field 7 is now the named gear, so it is unknown only when its value is outside the definitions; that raw value is still shown, for example unknownFields=[7:varint=9(0x9)], because that value is outside the definitions). Every other field and frame keeps the number-only form.
  • A missing enum value cannot be told from its zero value: an absent lock state reads VEHICLELOCKSTATE_UNLOCKED (sleep and presence read UNKNOWN). Only message parts can be reported as absent.
  • Encrypted content and session info appear by length only, public keys by SHA-1 prefix only. The VIN and decrypted content never appear. Keep this rule when you add lines.
  • The BLE transport counts inbox overflows per connection. Each dropped message logs Dropping received message because inbox is full (dropped N since connect) at ERROR; when the link ends, one line BLE inbox overflowed N time(s) during this connection is logged at WARN (only if N is above 0).
  • DebugLogger also prints the raw BLE bytes (TX: and RX:) at DEBUG. Do not share such platform logs. Only what the car encrypted stays encrypted there. Everything else appears as the car sent it, including VCSEC query replies, broadcasts and a plaintext infotainment reply (firmware before 2024.38, see 16. Error Handling), which can hold the location.

19. Architecture

A summary for contributors: how the modules depend on each other, the path a command takes, the concurrency rules, and how sessions are cached. The full design is docs/sdd/SDD.md. This page covers M0 to M3 and the broadcast path behind vehicle.events, and is extended when the control commands land.

On this page
  1. Modules
  2. The path of a command
  3. Broadcasts and vehicle.events
  4. Concurrency
  5. Bluetooth adapter
  6. Facade
  7. Session cache
  8. Protocol definitions

Modules

  • Every dependency points towards :domain, and the Gradle convention plugins enforce it.
  • The adapters implement the ports of :domain for each platform; :sdk wires them together.
  • :testing holds fakes such as FakeVehicle and FakeTransport and is used only by tests.

The path of a command

Using a security-controller command such as lock() as the example:

  1. Serialize. VcsecCommands takes the per-connection lock, so only one VCSEC command is in flight.
  2. Retry loop. Each attempt that fails with a retryable error starts again after the transport's retry interval.
  3. Sign and encrypt. Dispatcher.send asks SessionState to authorize the message: the counter goes up, the metadata is bound, a nonce is drawn.
  4. Send. The bytes go to the Transport port, implemented by :adapter-ble. Just before the first Bluetooth write starts, the transport tells the dispatcher, and from then on the request counts as possibly sent: a timeout is Uncertain, even if the write never finished (16, Retries and timeouts). If a write fails, the dispatcher writes the same bytes again and marks the request; a marked command then gets no newly signed copy, because the car may already have run the first one.
  5. Wait. The request waits until an answer is judged final. For a marked command, an answer that would mean a retry, or a refusal of the message itself, may be about the duplicate, so it keeps waiting on the same request until the time limit. Reads keep the normal retry.

Infotainment commands take the same steps from the retry loop on, without the lock. On the way back, one receive coroutine per connection reads Transport.incoming, parses each message, matches it to the waiting request, applies attached session information, decrypts it and checks it against replays, then hands it over.

Broadcasts and vehicle.events

The car also sends frames that answer no request. The Go original drops them; this library keeps them out of reply matching too, but passes the plain ones on (FR-035):

  1. Drop from routing. A frame addressed to a domain instead of a routing address gets the drop reason TO_DOMAIN and an observation log line, as before. It never reaches a waiting request.
  2. Gate. If it comes from the security domain, goes to the broadcast domain and has no request UUID, signature or session info, it is a plain broadcast.
  3. Offer. The receive coroutine offers a copy to Dispatcher.plainBroadcasts with tryEmit: no replay, room for 64 frames, never waiting. When it is full, the new frame is dropped and counted, with a WARN on drops 1, 100, 200 and so on.
  4. Map. Each collector of Vehicle.events has its own subscription. VcsecBroadcasts turns each frame into Status, AuthenticationRequest or Unrecognized, with the same mapping as vehicleStatus(), in the collector's coroutine.
  5. End. The flow ends normally when connection becomes Disconnected for any reason or disconnect() is called.

Concurrency

  • Exactly one receive coroutine per connection. It never waits on another coroutine; it hands data over with non-blocking trySend (tryEmit for broadcasts, so a slow events collector cannot delay replies). The only wait: a new receive coroutine waits for the previous one to finish.
  • One VCSEC command at a time per connection. Waiting for the previous one counts against the timeout. Infotainment commands may run in parallel.
  • Each domain has one session state. Its lock covers only encrypting, decrypting and session updates, so waits are short. The cache write that reserves counters (see Session cache) happens outside that lock, in the caller's coroutine, before the request's Bluetooth write.
  • The handshakes for both domains run in parallel; if one fails, the other is cancelled.
  • Commands, handshakes and connections each have their own timeout; see 16. Error Handling.

Bluetooth adapter

Only two classes know Kable: KableGattConnector (scan, open) and KableGattLink (write, subscribe, notice drops). BleTransportFactory and BleTransport do framing, reassembly, retries and error mapping without Kable, so they are tested on the JVM. When the link drops, the incoming flow closes and the state becomes Disconnected. The scan matches the advertised local name exactly.

Facade

TeslaBle.create assembles the platform adapters: Bluetooth transport, key store, session cache and crypto primitives. connect opens the transport, creates a coroutine scope for that connection, attaches a Dispatcher and a VehicleSession, and restores saved sessions. If anything fails or is cancelled on the way, the transport and the scope are always released. One Vehicle stands for one connection; after disconnect() its calls fail with NotConnected. The dispatcher's broadcast flow is passed to the Vehicle, whose events maps it and ends with the connection.

Session cache

Sessions are saved in a small binary format (version 1) so the next connection can send commands without a new handshake. They are tied to the client key (SHA-1 of its public key); sessions of other keys are ignored and deleted. They are saved when a handshake finishes, when session information changes, and on disconnect. Private keys, session encryption keys and raw VINs are never saved. Damaged or unrecognized entries are skipped and the rest restored.

Counter reservation (ADR-0016, differs from Go). The saved anti-replay counter is written before it is used, so an app that ends without disconnect() (force quit, the system ending a suspended app, a crash) does not leave the next launch a counter the car has already accepted. The Go command-line tool saves the real counter when it exits, which works only for a clean exit.

TopicRule
PromiseA saved entry's counter is at least the highest counter used in that epoch, provided the cache write succeeded
When it writesBefore a signed request uses a counter the cache does not hold yet: the library saves that part of the car's counter plus 16 (stopping at 0xFFFFFFFF), then signs. One write covers the next 16 requests. The check and the signing happen under the session state's lock; if parallel requests use up a fresh reservation, or the session or epoch changed while it was being written, it reserves again
Other savesNever write a counter below the saved reservation, including the save on disconnect
ValidityA reservation belongs to one session and its epoch. A new, replaced or outdated session, or an epoch change, ends it; session information for the same epoch keeps it
When the write failsThe request is sent as before and a warning is logged. A write the cache only logged as failed (the built-in caches do this) counts as saved. A connection that is closing returns NotConnected without using a counter
Counter limitA saved entry with fewer than 16 counters left is not restored (a handshake follows). A session that used 0xFFFFFFFF answers the next signed call with NoSession; startSession() builds a new one, only for the same car key. If the car never accepted that last counter, the new session starts from the car's lower counter in the same epoch (none of the skipped counters was accepted). Until then the old session still decrypts answers to requests already sent
Not coveredA failed write the cache only logged, another app or a second live connection using the same key (including an old dropped connection whose disconnect() finishes after a new connection reserved), and a cache saved by an older build (its first request can still be refused once and recovered)
CostOne cache write before the first signed request of a connection, then one per 16 requests. The car sees the counter jump by usually 16 or less on the first request after an unclean exit or a reconnect; the Go reference accepts forward jumps, a real-car check is pending

Protocol definitions

The :domain module generates Kotlin from 9 .proto files with Wire 7.0.4. Since 2026-10-18 these files are an unmodified copy of Teslemetry/tesla-protocol at a pinned commit (e6f7afb1, Apache-2.0). That snapshot holds the official vehicle-command definitions plus additions marked TESLEMETRY-EXT; some are also marked UNCONFIRMED or name unverified. The sources are recorded in NOTICE and PROTO_SOURCE.md.

TopicRule
What counts as correctThe official Go code (vehicle-command a4b43c1) for meaning, cryptography, routing and errors. If the two disagree, the Go code wins.
Added fields and valuesThey exist as generated types only. No command, event or public value uses them until a real car or the Go code confirms them. KeyRole and KeyFormFactor read them as UNKNOWN. Confirmed by real-car captures and therefore public: VehicleStatus.gear, and authenticationRequest as VehicleEvent.AuthenticationRequest (its sub-field names stay unverified).
UpdatingCheck out tesla-protocol at the pinned commit, run tools/proto/sync-protos.sh <checkout> (no network; it refuses any other commit), regenerate, then run apiDump and the tests. Change the pinned commit in the script and in docs/PATHS.md in the same PR.
